.

5.2
Function spaces

We now review some function spaces which appear in the following sections, together with a few notations.

Hereafter, the half line of non-negative real numbers is denoted
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. The space of real square-integrable functions over
\mathbb{R}_{+}
is denoted
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and is equipped with the usual scalar product:

\braket{f,g}=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}{f(x)g(x)dx},

(22)

for
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
.
This space is isomorphic to the space of square-summable real sequences indexed by
\mathbb{N}
,
l^{2}(\mathbb{N})
, by considering the expansion in a countable basis. Such a basis is given, e.g., by the Laguerre functions
[
62
]
,
modified here by a
(-1)^{k}
prefactor to correspond to Fock state Wigner functions:

\mathcal{L}_{k}(x):=(-1)^{k}L_{k}(x)e^{-\frac{x}{2}},

(23)

for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and all
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, where
L_{k}(x)=\sum_{l=0}^{k}\frac{(-1)^{l}}{l!}\binom{k}{l}x^{l}
is the
k^{\text{th}}
Laguerre polynomial.
These functions form an orthonormal basis: for all
p,q\in\mathbb{N}
,
\braket{\mathcal{L}_{p},\mathcal{L}_{q}}=\delta_{pq}
, where
\delta_{pq}
is the Kronecker symbol.

The space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
is also isomorphic to its dual space
{L^{2}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
via
the Radon–Nikodym theorem
[
63
]
:
elements of
{L^{2}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
can be identified by the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
times the corresponding function in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
.

We write
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
the space of Schwartz functions over
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, i.e., the space of
C^{\infty}
functions that go to
0
at infinity faster than any inverse polynomial, as do their derivatives.
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
is its dual space, the space of tempered distributions over
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
Note that
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
is dense in
{L^{2}}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
.
We denote the space of rapidly decreasing real sequences by
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{N})
(sequences that go to
0
at infinity faster than any inverse polynomial), together with its dual space of slowly increasing real sequences
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{N})
(sequences that are upper bounded by a polynomial). The spaces
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{N})
are isomorphic: any Schwartz function over
\mathbb{R}_{+}
can be expanded uniquely in the basis of Laguerre functions with a rapidly decreasing sequence of coefficients. Similarly, the spaces
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{N})
are also isomorphic: any tempered distribution over
\mathbb{R}_{+}
can be written uniquely as a formal series of Laguerre functions with a slowly increasing sequence of coefficients
[
64
]
.
We extend the definition of the duality
\braket{\text{--},\text{--}}
in Eq. (
22
) to these spaces.

In order to denote non-negative elements of these spaces, we will use the notations
L^{2}_{+}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
,
{L_{+}^{2}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
,
\mathcal{S}_{+}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and
\mathcal{S}_{+}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
. A distribution
\mu
in
{L_{+}^{2}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
(resp. in
\mathcal{S}_{+}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
) satisfies:
\forall f\in L^{2}_{+}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
(resp.
\forall f\in\mathcal{S}_{+}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
),
\braket{\mu,f}\geq 0
.

For all
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the following space of truncated series of Laguerre functions over
\mathbb{R}_{+}
:

\mathcal{R}_{m}(\mathbb{R}_{+}):=\text{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{\mathcal{L}_{k}\}_{0\leq k\leq m},

(24)

which is equal to the set of real polynomials over
\mathbb{R}_{+}
of degree less or equal to
m
multiplied by the function
x\mapsto e^{-\frac{x}{2}}
. We denote by
\mathcal{R}_{m,+}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
its subset of non-negative elements.

For all
\bm{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
, we define the associated formal series of Laguerre functions:

f_{\bm{s}}:=\sum_{k\geq 0}s_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k},

(25)

with the (formal) relation:

s_{k}=\braket{f_{\bm{s}},\mathcal{L}_{k}},

(26)

for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. We refer to
\bm{s}
as the sequence of Laguerre moments of
f_{\bm{s}}
. We extend this definition to finite sequences by completing these sequences with zeros.
For
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we also define the matrix
A_{\bm{s}}
(thus omitting the dependence in
m
) by

(A_{\bm{s}})_{0\leq i,j\leq m}:=\begin{cases}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{l}s_{k}\binom{l}{k}l!&\text{if }i+j=2l,\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(27)

A_{\bm{s}}
can be seen as the Laguerre moment matrix of the measure
f_{\bm{s}}
.
In what follows, we use standard techniques relating to the Stieltjes moment problem
[
65
]
, which seeks conditions for a real sequence
\bm{\nu}=(\nu_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
to be the sequence of moments
\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}x^{k}d\nu(x)
of a non-negative distribution
\nu
over
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. We adapt these techniques to the basis of Laguerre functions, rather than the canonical basis. In particular, we make use of the following result:

Theorem 1
.

Let
\bm{\mu}=(\mu_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
. The sequence
\bm{\mu}
is the sequence of Laguerre moments
\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\mathcal{L}_{k}(x)d\mu(x)
of a non-negative distribution
\mu
supported on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
if and only if

\forall m\in\mathbb{N},\forall g\in\mathcal{R}_{m,+}(\mathbb{R}_{+}),\;\braket{f_{\bm{\mu}},g}\geq 0.

(28)

We give a proof in Appendix
A
for this result, which is based on the classic Riesz–Haviland theorem
[
66
,
67
]
.

5.3
Linear program

In this section, we phrase the computation of the witness threshold value introduced in Eq. (
8
) as an infinite-dimensional linear program, in the case where one entry of the vector
\bm{a}
is equal to
1
and all the other entries are
0
, the generalisation being straightforward by linearity.

Formally, we fix hereafter
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and we look for the witnesses threshold value
\omega_{n}

3
3

3

Here we write generically the threshold value as
\omega_{n}
while we use the more precise notation
\omega_{n}^{L^{2}}
(resp.
\omega_{n}^{\mathcal{S}}
) to refer to its computation in the space of square-integrable functions (resp. Schwartz functions).

defined as

\omega_{n}:=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})\\
W_{\rho}\geq 0\end{subarray}}\braket{n|\rho|n}.

(29)

This is the maximal values such that for all states
\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
:

\braket{n|\rho|n}>\omega_{n}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\exists\alpha\in\mathbb{C},\;W_{\rho}(\alpha)<0.

(30)

Let
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H})
be the set of states that are invariant under phase-space rotations:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H})\!:=\!\{\sigma

\displaystyle\in\!\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})\text{ such that:}

(31)

\displaystyle\forall\varphi\in[0,2\pi],{\mathrm{e}}^{{\mathrm{i}}\varphi\hat{n}}\sigma{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\varphi\hat{n}}\!=\!\sigma\},

where
\hat{n}=\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}
is the number operator.
The witnesses corresponding to the fidelity with a single Fock state feature a rotational symmetry in phase space, which we exploit in the following lemma.

Lemma 2
.

The threshold value in Eq. (
29
) can be expressed as

\omega_{n}=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H})\\
W_{\sigma}\geq 0\end{subarray}}\braket{n|\sigma|n}.

(32)

Proof.

Let
\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
. We start by applying a random dephasing to the state
\rho
:

\sigma=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\text{d}\varphi}{2\pi}{\mathrm{e}}^{{\mathrm{i}}\varphi\hat{n}}\rho{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\varphi\hat{n}}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}).

(33)

The random dephasing does not change the fidelity with any Fock state because of the rotational symmetry in phase space of the latter, i.e.,

\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\,\braket{n|\sigma|n}=\braket{n|\rho|n}.

(34)

Moreover, it can only decrease the maximum negativity of the Wigner function: for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
,

\displaystyle W_{\sigma}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\text{d}\varphi}{2\pi}W_{e^{{\mathrm{i}}\varphi\hat{n}}\rho e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\varphi\hat{n}}}(\alpha)

(35)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\text{d}\varphi}{2\pi}W_{\rho}(\alpha{\mathrm{e}}^{{\mathrm{i}}\varphi})

\displaystyle\geq\min_{\varphi\in[0,2\pi]}W_{\rho}(\alpha{\mathrm{e}}^{{\mathrm{i}}\varphi})

\displaystyle\geq\min_{\beta\in\mathbb{C}}W_{\rho}(\beta),

and taking the minimum over all
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
then gives

\min_{\alpha\in\mathbb{C}}W_{\sigma}(\alpha)\geq\min_{\beta\in\mathbb{C}}W_{\rho}(\beta).

(36)

In particular, applying a random dephasing to a Wigner positive state yields a Wigner positive mixtures of Fock states, which is invariant under phase-space rotations. Hence, we can restrict without loss of generality to states that are invariant under phase-space rotations when looking for the maximum fidelity of Wigner positive states with a given Fock state
\ket{n}
.
∎

Lemma
2
ensures that the supremum in Eq. (
29
) can be computed over states that have a rotational symmetry in phase space. Such states
\sigma
can be expanded diagonally in the Fock basis:

\sigma=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}F_{k}\ket{k}\!\bra{k},

(37)

with
\sum_{k}F_{k}=1
and
0\leq F_{k}\leq 1
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
By linearity of the Wigner function:

\forall\alpha\in\mathbb{C},\;W_{\sigma}(\alpha)=\sum_{k}F_{k}W_{k}(\alpha),

(38)

where
W_{k}
is the Wigner function of the
k^{th}
Fock state
[
20
]
:

\forall\alpha\in\mathbb{C},\,W_{k}(\alpha)=\frac{2}{\pi}\mathcal{L}_{k}(4|\alpha|^{2}),

(39)

with
\mathcal{L}_{k}
the
k^{\text{th}}
Laguerre function, defined in Eq. (
23
). As noted before, Fock states are invariant under phase-space rotations: their Wigner function only depends on the amplitude of the phase-space point considered. We fix
x=4|\alpha|^{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
hereafter.

As we consider the optimisation over
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
functions, we will denote the corresponding threshold value by
\omega_{n}^{L^{2}}
. With Lemma
2
, the computation of
\omega_{n}^{L^{2}}
can thus be expressed as the following infinite-dimensional linear program:

\displaystyle\text{Find }(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})

(LP
{}_{n}^{L^{2}}
)

\displaystyle\text{maximising }F_{n}

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\sum_{k}F_{k}=1\\
\forall k\in\mathbb{N},\;F_{k}\geq 0\rule[-9.60004pt]{0.0pt}{9.60004pt}\\
\forall x\in\mathbb{R}_{+},\;\sum_{k}F_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k}(x)\geq 0.\end{cases}

The first constraint ensures unit trace of the corresponding state
\sigma
, the second one ensures that its fidelity with each Fock state is non-negative, and the last one ensures that its Wigner function
W_{\sigma}
is non-negative. Note that
\omega_{n}>0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, by considering a mixture of
\ket{0}
and
\ket{n}
with the vacuum component close enough to
1
. Note that, for consistency of notations, we use
L
instead of
\ell
in the label of the program.

We refer the interested reader to Appendix
B

where this program is expressed in the canonical form of infinite-dimensional linear programs
[
61
]
and its dual is derived. This dual linear program reads:

\displaystyle\text{Find }y\in\mathbb{R}\text{ and }\mu\in{L^{2}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+})

(D-LP
{}_{n}^{L^{2}}
)

\displaystyle\text{minimising }y

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\forall k\neq n\in\mathbb{N},\;y\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}{\mathcal{L}_{k}}{d\mu}\\
y\geq 1+\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}{\mathcal{L}_{n}}{d\mu}\rule[-9.60004pt]{0.0pt}{9.60004pt}\\
\forall f\in L^{2}_{+}(\mathbb{R}_{+}),\;\langle\mu,f\rangle\geq 0.\end{cases}

We also consider a restriction of
(LP

n

L
2

)
by optimising over Schwartz functions rather than square-integrable functions as
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\subset L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
. The primal program becomes:

\displaystyle\text{Find }(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{N})

(LP
{}_{n}^{\mathcal{S}}
)

\displaystyle\text{maximising }F_{n}

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\sum_{k}F_{k}=1\\
\forall k\in\mathbb{N},\;F_{k}\geq 0\rule[-9.60004pt]{0.0pt}{9.60004pt}\\
\forall x\in\mathbb{R}_{+},\;\sum_{k}F_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k}(x)\geq 0,\end{cases}

We denote its value by
\omega_{n}^{\mathcal{S}}
.
Because it is a restriction of
(LP

n

L
2

)
, we have that
\omega_{n}^{L^{2}}\geq\omega_{n}^{\mathcal{S}}
.
Its dual linear program can be expressed as:

\displaystyle\text{Find }y\in\mathbb{R}\text{ and }\mu\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+})

(D-LP
{}_{n}^{\mathcal{S}}
)

\displaystyle\text{minimising }y

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\forall k\neq n\in\mathbb{N},\;y\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}{\mathcal{L}_{k}}{d\mu}\\
y\geq 1+\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}{\mathcal{L}_{n}}{d\mu}\rule[-9.60004pt]{0.0pt}{9.60004pt}\\
\forall f\in\mathcal{S}_{+}(\mathbb{R}_{+}),\;\langle\mu,f\rangle\geq 0.\end{cases}

We will show later that strong duality holds for the two pairs of programs.
However weak duality of linear programming already ensures that the optimal value
\omega_{n}^{L^{2}}
of
(LP

n

L
2

)
(resp.
\omega_{n}^{\mathcal{S}}
of
(LP

n

S

)
) is upper bounded by the optimal value of
(D-LP

n

L
2

)
(resp.
(D-LP

n

S

)
). Hence, a possible way of solving the optimisation
(LP

n

L
2

)
is to exhibit a feasible solution for
(LP

n

L
2

)
and a feasible solution for
(D-LP

n

L
2

)
that have the same value.

For
n=1
, choosing
(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}=(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},0,0,\dots)
gives a feasible solution for (LP
{}_{1}^{L^{2}}
)
(resp. (LP
{}_{1}^{\mathcal{S}}
))
with the value
\frac{1}{2}
, while choosing
(y,\mu)=(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\delta(x))
, where
\delta
is the Dirac delta function
4
4

4

Technically
\delta\notin{L^{2}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, but the result holds by considering a sequence of functions converging to a Dirac delta.

over
\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
gives a feasible solution for (D-LP
{}_{1}^{L^{2}}
)
(resp. (D-LP
{}_{1}^{\mathcal{S}}
))
with the value
\frac{1}{2}
.
This shows that
\omega_{1}^{L^{S}}=\omega_{1}^{\mathcal{S}}=\frac{1}{2}
.

Similarly, for
n=2
, choosing
(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}=(\frac{1}{2},0,\frac{1}{2},0,0,\dots)
gives a feasible solution for (LP
{}_{2}^{L^{2}}
)
(resp. LP
{}_{2}^{\mathcal{S}}
)
with the value
\frac{1}{2}
, while choosing
(y,\mu)=(\frac{1}{2},\frac{e}{2}\delta(x-2))
gives a feasible solution for (D-LP
2
)
(resp. (D-LP
{}_{2}^{\mathcal{S}}
))
, up to a conjecture
5
5

5

We checked numerically the corresponding constraints
|L_{k}(2)|\leq 1
for
k
up to
10^{3}
and, considering asymptotic behaviors, we conjecture that these hold for all
k\geq 0
.
, with the value
\frac{1}{2}
. This shows that
\omega_{2}^{L^{2}}=\omega_{2}^{S}=\frac{1}{2}
.

While this approach is sensible for small values of
n
, finding optimal analytical solutions for higher values of
n
seems highly nontrivial. Moreover, the infinite number of variables prevents us from performing the optimisation
(LP

n

L
2

)
numerically. A natural workaround is to find finite-dimensional relaxations or restrictions of the original problem—thus providing upper and lower bounds for the optimal value
\omega_{n}^{L^{2}}
, respectively. This is the approach we follow in the next section.

5.4
Hierarchies of semidefinite programs

Semidefinite programming is a convex optimisation technique in the cone of positive semidefinite matrices. It comes with a duality theory: if a primal semidefinite program is a maximisation problem, then one may deduce a dual minimisation problem which is again a semidefinite program. Like linear programs, these programs satisfy a weak duality condition: the optimal value of the primal problem is upper bounded by the optimal value of the dual program. The difference between the optimal values is called the duality gap. When there is no duality gap, we say that there is strong duality between the programs.

5.4.1
Preliminaries

In this section, we introduce preliminary technical lemmas and we refer the reader to Appendix
C
for their proofs.

We recall the following standard result, which comes from the fact that any univariate polynomial non-negative over
\mathbb{R}
can be written as a sum of squares:

Lemma 3

(
[
68
]
)
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{N}
and let
P
be a univariate polynomial of degree
2p
. Let
X=(1,x,\dots,x^{p})
be the vector of monomials. Then,
P
is non-negative over
\mathbb{R}
if and only if there exists a real
(p+1)\times(p+1)
positive semidefinite matrix
Q
such that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

P(x)=X^{T}QX.

(40)

From this lemma we deduce the following characterisation of non-negative polynomials over
\mathbb{R}_{+}
:

Lemma 4
.

Non-negative polynomials on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
can be written as sums of polynomials of the form
\sum_{l=0}^{p}x^{l}\sum_{i+j=2l}y_{i}y_{j}
, where
p\in\mathbb{N}
and
y_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
, for all
0\leq i\leq p
.

Note that the characterisation in Lemma
4
differs from that of Stieltjes
[
65
]
, which expresses non-negative polynomials over
\mathbb{R}_{+}
as
x\mapsto A_{1}(x)+xA_{2}(x)
, where
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
are sums of squares.
This slightly slows down the numerical resolution, which does not matter given the size of the programs considered. At the same time, this allows us to obtain more compact expressions for the semidefinite programs.

We use the characterisation of Lemma
4
to obtain the following result: for
\bm{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
, the fact that the series
f_{\bm{s}}
defined in Eq. (
25
) has non-negative scalar product with non-negative truncated Laguerre series up to degree
m
can be expressed as a positive semidefinite constraint involving the matrix
A_{\bm{s}}
defined in Eq. (
27
). Formally:

Lemma 5
.

Let
m\geq n
and let
\bm{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
. The following propositions are equivalent:

(i)

\forall g\in\mathcal{R}_{m,+}(\mathbb{R}_{+}),\;\braket{f_{\bm{s}},g}\geq 0
,

(ii)

A_{\bm{s}}\succeq 0
.

Using these results, we derive hierarchies of semidefinite relaxations and restrictions for the infinite-dimensional linear program
(LP

n

L
2

)
in the following sections.

5.4.2
Semidefinite relaxations

One way to obtain a relaxation of
(LP

n

L
2

)
is to relax the constraint:

\forall x\in\mathbb{R}_{+},\;f_{\bm{F}}(x)=\sum_{k}F_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k}(x)\geq 0.

(41)

Instead, one may impose the weaker constraint:

\forall g\in\mathcal{R}_{m,+}(\mathbb{R}_{+}),\;\braket{f_{\bm{F}},g}\geq 0,

(42)

for some fixed
m\geq n
. By Lemma
5
, this constraint may in turn be expressed as a positive semidefinite constraint on the
(m+1)\times(m+1)
matrix
A_{\bm{F}}
defined in Eq. (
27
).

Each choice of
m
thus leads to a different semidefinite program, whose optimal value gets closer to
\omega_{n}
as
m
increases (since the constraint (
42
) gets stronger when
m
increases). Moreover, when replacing the constraint (
41
) by the constraint (
42
) in
(LP

n

L
2

)
, for
l>m
the variables
F_{l}
do not appear in the matrix
A_{\bm{F}}
, and are constrained only by
\sum_{k}F_{k}=1
and
F_{l}\geq 0
. Since
m\geq n
and the optimal value is
F_{n}
, we may thus set
F_{l}=0
for
l>m
without loss of generality.
This gives a hierarchy of finite-dimensional semidefinite relaxations for
(LP

n

L
2

)
, and the semidefinite relaxation of order
m
is given by:

\displaystyle\text{Find }A\in\text{Sym}_{m+1}\text{ and }\bm{F}\in\mathbb{R}^{m+1}

(\text{SDP}^{m,\geq}_{n})

\displaystyle\text{maximising }F_{n}

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\textstyle\sum_{k=0}^{m}F_{k}=1\\
\textstyle\forall k\leq m,\;F_{k}\geq 0\\
\textstyle\forall l\leq m,\forall i+j=2l,\;A_{ij}=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{l}F_{k}\binom{l}{k}l!\hskip-85.35826pt\\
\textstyle\forall l\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket,\forall i+j=2l-1,\;A_{ij}=0\hskip-85.35826pt\\
\textstyle A\succeq 0.\end{cases}

Let us denote its optimal value by
\omega^{m,\geq}_{n}
. The sequence
\{\omega^{m,\geq}_{n}\}_{m\geq n}
is a decreasing sequence and for all
m\geq n
, we have
\omega_{n}^{L^{2}}\leq\omega^{m,\geq}_{n}
.

For each
m\geq n
, the program

(

SDP

m
,
≥

n

)

has a dual semidefinite program which is given by (see Appendix
D
for a detailed derivation):

\displaystyle\text{Find }Q\in\text{Sym}_{m+1},\bm{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}^{m+1}\text{ and }y\in\mathbb{R}\hskip-85.35826pt

(\text{D-SDP}^{m,\geq}_{n})

\displaystyle\text{minimising }y

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\textstyle y\geq 1+\mu_{n}\\
\textstyle\forall k\leq m,\;y\geq\mu_{k}\\
\textstyle\forall l\leq m,\sum\limits_{i+j=2l}Q_{ij}=\textstyle\sum\limits_{k=l}^{m}\frac{(-1)^{k+l}}{l!}\binom{k}{l}\mu_{k}\hskip-56.9055pt\\
\textstyle Q\succeq 0.\end{cases}

We show in Theorem
3
that strong duality holds between the primal and the dual versions of this semidefinite program. In particular, numerical computations with either of these programs will yield the same optimal value.

5.4.3
Semidefinite restrictions

A trivial way to obtain a restriction of
(LP

n

L
2

)
(or equivalently of
(LP

n

S

)
) is to impose
F_{l}=0
for
l>m
, for some
m\geq n
. What is less trivial is that this yields a finite-dimensional semidefinite program.
Indeed, the constraint (
41
) becomes

\forall x\in\mathbb{R}_{+},\;\sum_{k=0}^{m}F_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k}(x)\geq 0,

(43)

or equivalently:

\forall x\in\mathbb{R},\;\sum_{k=0}^{m}(-1)^{k}F_{k}L_{k}(x^{2})\geq 0,

(44)

where we used Eq. (
23
). By Lemma
3
, writing
X=(1,x,\dots,x^{m})
, this is equivalent to the existence of a positive semidefinite matrix
Q=(Q_{ij})_{0\leq i,j\leq m}
such that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m}(-1)^{k}F_{k}L_{k}(x^{2})

\displaystyle=X^{T}QX

(45)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{m}x^{l}\sum_{i+j=l}Q_{ij}.

This is in turn equivalent to the linear constraints:

\displaystyle\forall l\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket,\;\sum\limits_{i+j=2l-1}Q_{ij}=0,

(46)

\displaystyle\text{and}

\displaystyle\forall l\leq m,\sum\limits_{i+j=2l}Q_{ij}=\frac{(-1)^{l}}{l!}\sum\limits_{k=l}^{m}(-1)^{k}\binom{k}{l}F_{k},\hskip-28.45274pt

by identifying the coefficients in front of each monomial in Eq. (
45
).

Hence, the restriction of
(LP

n

L
2

)
obtained by imposing
F_{l}=0
for
l>m
, for a fixed
m\geq n
, is a semidefinite program given by:

\displaystyle\text{Find }Q\in\text{Sym}_{m+1}\text{ and }\bm{F}\in\mathbb{R}^{m+1}

(\text{SDP}^{m,\leq}_{n})

\displaystyle\text{maximising }F_{n}

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\textstyle\sum_{k=0}^{m}F_{k}=1\\
\textstyle\forall k\leq m,\;F_{k}\geq 0\\
\textstyle\forall l\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket,\;\sum\limits_{i+j=2l-1}Q_{ij}=0\hskip-56.9055pt\\
\textstyle\forall l\leq m,\sum\limits_{i+j=2l}Q_{ij}=\sum\limits_{k=l}^{m}\frac{(-1)^{k+l}}{l!}\binom{k}{l}F_{k}\hskip-56.9055pt\\
\textstyle Q\succeq 0.\end{cases}

Let us denote its optimal value by
\omega^{m,\leq}_{n}
.
Each choice of
m
leads to a different semidefinite restriction of
(LP

n

L
2

)
and
(LP

n

S

)
, whose optimal value gets closer to
\omega_{n}^{\mathcal{S}}
as
m
increases (since the constraint
F_{l}=0
for
l>m
gets weaker when
m
increases). The sequence
\{\omega^{m,\leq}_{n}\}_{m\geq n}
is thus an increasing sequence and for all
m\geq n
, we have
\omega^{m,\leq}_{n}\leq\omega_{n}^{\mathcal{S}}\leq\omega_{n}^{L^{2}}
.

For each
m\geq n
, the program

(

SDP

m
,
≤

n

)

has a dual semidefinite program which is given by (see Appendix
D
for a detailed derivation):

\displaystyle\text{Find }A\in\text{Sym}_{m+1},\bm{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}^{m+1}\text{ and }y\in\mathbb{R}\hskip-56.9055pt

(\text{D-SDP}^{m,\leq}_{n})

\displaystyle\text{minimising }y

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\textstyle y\geq 1+\mu_{n}\\
\textstyle\forall k\leq m,\;y\geq\mu_{k}\\
\textstyle\forall l\leq m,\forall i+j=2l,\;A_{ij}=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{l}\mu_{k}\binom{l}{k}l!\hskip-56.9055pt\\
\textstyle A\succeq 0.\end{cases}

We show in Theorem
2
that strong duality holds between the primal and the dual versions of this semidefinite program.

5.4.4
Strong duality of semidefinite programs

We fix
m\geq n
and we show that strong duality holds both for the semidefinite restriction

(

SDP

m
,
≤

n

)

and the semidefinite relaxation

(

SDP

m
,
≥

n

)

.

We first consider the semidefinite restrictions:

Theorem 2
.

Strong duality holds between the programs

(

SDP

m
,
≤

n

)

and

(

D-SDP

m
,
≤

n

)

.

Proof.

We make use of Slater condition for (finite-dimensional) semidefinite programs: strict feasibility of

(

SDP

m
,
≤

n

)

implies strong duality between

(

SDP

m
,
≤

n

)

and

(

D-SDP

m
,
≤

n

)

.

In order to obtain a strictly feasible solution, we define
Q:=\frac{1}{2^{m+1}-1}\text{Diag}_{k=0,\dots,m}(\frac{1}{k!})\in\text{Sym}_{m+1}
and
\bm{F}=(F_{0},\dots,F_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
, where for all
k\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket
,
F_{k}:=\frac{1}{2^{m+1}-1}\binom{m+1}{k+1}
.
Then
Q\succ 0
and
F_{k}>0
for all
k\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket
. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m}F_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{m+1}-1}\sum_{k=0}^{m}\binom{m+1}{k+1}

(47)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{m+1}-1}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{m+1}\binom{m+1}{k}-1\right)

\displaystyle=1.

We also have
\sum_{i+j=2l-1}Q_{ij}=0
for all
l\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
, since
Q
is diagonal. Furthermore, for all
l\leq m
,

\sum_{i+j=2l}Q_{ij}=Q_{ll}=\frac{1}{2^{m+1}-1}\frac{1}{l!},

(48)

and

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{l}}{l!}\sum\limits_{k=l}^{m}(-1)^{k}\binom{k}{l}F_{k}

(49)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{m+1}-1}\frac{1}{l!}\sum_{k=l}^{m}(-1)^{k-l}\binom{k}{m}\binom{m+1}{k+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{m+1}-1}\frac{1}{l!}\binom{m}{l}\sum_{q=0}^{m-l}(-1)^{q}\frac{m+1}{q+l+1}\binom{m-l}{q}\hskip-28.45274pt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{m+1}-1}\frac{1}{l!},

where we used
[
69
, (1.41)]
in the last line.
Therefore,
(Q,\bm{F})
is a strictly feasible solution of

(

SDP

m
,
≤

n

)

, which implies strong duality.
∎

As a consequence of the proof of Theorem
2
, we also obtain strong duality for the semidefinite relaxations:

Theorem 3
.

Strong duality holds between the programs

(

SDP

m
,
≥

n

)

and

(

D-SDP

m
,
≥

n

)

.

Proof.

The program

(

SDP

m
,
≥

n

)

is a relaxation of
(LP

n

L
2

)
and

(

SDP

m
,
≤

n

)

is a restriction of
(LP

n

L
2

)
, so

(

SDP

m
,
≥

n

)

is a relaxation of

(

SDP

m
,
≤

n

)

. Hence, the strictly feasible solution of

(

SDP

m
,
≤

n

)

derived in the proof of Theorem
2
yields a strictly feasible solution
(A,\bm{F})
for

(

SDP

m
,
≥

n

)

: we set

\bm{F}=(F_{0},\dots,F_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
, where for all
k\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket
,
F_{k}:=\frac{1}{2^{m+1}-1}\binom{m+1}{k+1}
and
A=A_{\bm{F}}\in\text{Sym}_{m+1}
, where
A_{\bm{F}}
is defined in Eq. (
27
).

With Slater condition, this shows again that strong duality holds between the programs

(

SDP

m
,
≥

n

)

and

(

D-SDP

m
,
≥

n

)

.
∎

5.5
Convergence of the hierarchies of semidefinite programs

From the previous sections, for
m\geq n
the optimal values
\omega^{m,\geq}_{n}
and
\omega^{m,\leq}_{n}
of

(

SDP

m
,
≥

n

)

and

(

SDP

m
,
≤

n

)

form decreasing and increasing sequences, respectively, which satisfy

0\leq\omega^{m,\leq}_{n}\leq\omega_{n}^{\mathcal{S}}\leq\omega_{n}^{L^{2}}\leq\omega^{m,\geq}_{n}\leq 1.

(50)

Recall that
\omega_{n}^{L^{2}}
is the optimal value of
(LP

n

L
2

)
while
\omega_{n}^{\mathcal{S}}
is the optimal value of
(LP

n

S

)
. These sequences thus both converge, and the remaining question is whether
(\omega^{m,\geq}_{n})_{m}
converges to
\omega_{n}^{L^{2}}
and
(\omega^{m,\leq}_{n})_{m}
converges to
\omega_{n}^{\mathcal{S}}
.
In this section, we show that this is indeed the case.

5.5.1
Convergence of the sequence of upper bounds

Theorem 4
.

The decreasing sequence of optimal values of

(

SDP

m
,
≥

n

)

converges to the optimal value of
(LP

n

L
2

)
:

\lim_{m\rightarrow+\infty}\omega^{m,\geq}_{n}=\omega_{n}^{L^{2}}.

(51)

In order to prove this theorem, we extract a limit from a sequence of optimal solutions of

(

SDP

m
,
≥

n

)

, for
m\geq n
, and we show using Theorem
1
that it provides a feasible solution of
(LP

n

L
2

)
.

Proof.

For all
m\geq n
, the feasible set of

(

SDP

m
,
≥

n

)

is non-empty (consider, e.g.,
\bm{F}=(1,0,0,\dots,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
). Moreover, due to the constraints
\sum_{k=0}^{m}F_{k}=1
and
F_{k}\geq 0
for all
k\leq m
, the feasible set of

(

SDP

m
,
≥

n

)

is compact. Hence, the program

(

SDP

m
,
≥

n

)

has feasible optimal solutions, for all
m\geq n
, by diagonal extraction.

The matrix
A
in

(

SDP

m
,
≥

n

)

is entirely fixed by the choice of
\bm{F}
. Let
(\bm{F}^{m})_{m\geq n}
be a sequence of optimal solutions of

(

SDP

m
,
≥

n

)

, for
m\geq n
.
For each
m\geq n
, we have by optimality that
F_{n}^{m}=\omega_{n}^{m,\geq}
, and the sequence
(F^{m}_{n})_{m\geq n}
converges. We complete each tuple
\bm{F}^{m}=(F_{0}^{m},F_{1}^{m},\dots,F_{m}^{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
with zeros to obtain a sequence in
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
, which we still denote
\bm{F}^{m}=(F_{0}^{m},F_{1}^{m},\dots,F_{m}^{m},0,0,\dots)\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
.

Performing a diagonal extraction
\phi
on the sequence of optimal solutions
(\bm{F}^{m})_{m\geq n}
, we obtain a sequence of sequences
(\bm{F}^{\phi(m)})_{m\geq n}
such that each sequence
(F^{\phi(m)}_{k})_{m\geq n}
converges when
m\rightarrow+\infty
, for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
F_{k}
denote its limit, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
. We write
\bm{F}=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
the sequence of limits.

For all
m\geq n
,
F_{k}^{\phi(m)}\geq 0
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\sum_{k}F_{k}^{\phi(m)}=1
, so taking
m\rightarrow+\infty
we obtain
F_{k}\geq 0
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\sum_{k}F_{k}\leq 1
. Moreover,

F_{n}=\lim_{m\rightarrow+\infty}F_{n}^{m}=\lim_{m\rightarrow+\infty}\omega^{m,\geq}_{n}.

(52)

For all
m\geq n
, we have
\omega^{m,\geq}_{n}\geq\omega_{n}^{L^{2}}
, so
F_{n}\geq\omega_{n}^{L^{2}}>0
. In particular,
\sum_{k}F_{k}>0
, so without loss of generality we may assume that
\sum_{k}F_{k}=1
(otherwise we can always replace
F_{k}
by
\frac{F_{k}}{\sum_{l}F_{l}}
).

Let
f_{\bm{F}}=\sum_{k}F_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
. By construction we have:

\forall m\geq n,\forall g\in\mathcal{R}_{m,+}(\mathbb{R}_{+}),\;\braket{f_{\bm{F}},g}\geq 0.

(53)

Hence, by Theorem
1
,
\bm{F}
is the sequence of Laguerre moments of a non-negative distribution over
\mathbb{R}_{+}
(the Lebesgue measure times the function
f_{\bm{F}}
). In particular,

\forall x\in\mathbb{R}_{+},\;f_{\bm{F}}(x)=\sum_{k}F_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k}(x)\geq 0.

(54)

With the constraints
F_{k}\geq 0
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\sum_{k}F_{k}\leq 1
, this implies that
\bm{F}
is a feasible solution of
(LP

n

L
2

)
, and in particular
F_{n}\leq\omega_{n}^{L^{2}}
, since
(LP

n

L
2

)
is a maximisation problem. Since we already had
F_{n}\geq\omega_{n}^{L^{2}}
we obtain with Eq. (
52
):

\lim_{m\rightarrow+\infty}\omega^{m,\geq}_{n}=\omega_{n}^{L^{2}},

(55)

which concludes the proof.
∎

This immediately implies strong duality for programs
(LP

n

L
2

)
and
(D-LP

n

L
2

)
because

(i)

we have weak duality between those programs so that the optimal value
\omega^{\prime L^{2}}_{n}
of
(D-LP

n

L
2

)
upper bounds the optimal value of
(LP

n

L
2

)

\omega^{L^{2}}_{n}
i.e.
\omega^{L^{2}}_{n}\leq\omega^{\prime L^{2}}_{n}
;

(ii)

we have strong duality between

(

SDP

m
,
≥

n

)

and

(

D-SDP

m
,
≥

n

)

by Theorem
3
;

(iii)

(

SDP

m
,
≥

n

)

is a relaxation of
(LP

n

L
2

)
so that
\forall m,\;\omega^{m,\geq}_{n}\geq\omega_{n}^{L^{2}}
;

(iv)

(

D-SDP

m
,
≥

n

)

is a restriction of
(D-LP

n

L
2

)
so that
\forall m,\omega^{m,\geq}_{n}\geq\omega^{\prime L^{2}}_{n}\geq\omega^{L^{2}}_{n}
;

(v)

we showed that the optimal value of the hierarchy

(

SDP

m
,
≥

n

)

converges to
\omega^{L^{2}}_{n}
i.e.
\lim_{m\rightarrow+\infty}\omega^{m,\geq}_{n}=\omega_{n}^{L^{2}}
.

5.5.2
Convergence of the sequence of lower bounds

Theorem 5
.

The increasing sequence of optimal values of

(

SDP

m
,
≤

n

)

converges to the optimal value of
(LP

n

S

)
:

\lim_{m\rightarrow+\infty}\omega^{m,\leq}_{n}=\omega_{n}^{\mathcal{S}}.

(56)

The proof is similar to that of Theorem
4
using the dual programs: we attempt to construct a feasible optimal solution of
(D-LP

n

L
2

)
by extracting a limit from a sequence of optimal solutions of

(

D-SDP

m
,
≤

n

)

, for
m\geq n
and then conclude using the strong duality between

(

SDP

m
,
≤

n

)

and

(

D-SDP

m
,
≤

n

)

, which we proved in Theorem
2
. However, it turns out that
(D-LP

n

L
2

)
may not have feasible optimal solutions in
{L^{2}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
(as anticipated with the analytical optimal solutions for
n=1
and
n=2
from section
5.3
).

To deal with this issue, we have extended the formulation of
(D-LP

n

L
2

)
to a larger space where it has feasible optimal solutions, namely the space of tempered distributions
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
(see
(D-LP

n

S

)
).

Note that the semidefinite restrictions

(

SDP

m
,
≤

n

)

of
(LP

n

L
2

)
are also restrictions of
(LP

n

S

)
for all
m\geq n
, while
(LP

n

S

)
is itself a restriction of
(LP

n

L
2

)
, and
(D-LP

n

S

)
is a relaxation of
(D-LP

n

L
2

)
. We denote by
{\omega_{n}^{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}}
the optimal value of
(D-LP

n

S

)
. Recall that the optimal value of
(LP

n

S

)
is denoted
\omega_{n}^{\mathcal{S}}
and that of
(LP

n

L
2

)
and
(D-LP

n

L
2

)
are denoted
\omega_{n}^{L^{2}}
(by strong duality). By weak duality of linear programming we thus have

\omega_{n}^{m,\leq}\leq\omega_{n}^{\mathcal{S}}\leq{\omega_{n}^{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}}\leq\omega_{n}^{L^{2}},

(57)

for all
m\geq n
(see also figure
4
).

Before proving Theorem
5
, we introduce two intermediate technical results. The first one is a reformulation of

(

D-SDP

m
,
≤

n

)

over Schwartz space:

Lemma 6
.

For all
m\geq n
, the program

(

D-SDP

m
,
≤

n

)

is equivalent to the following program:

\displaystyle\text{Find }y\in\mathbb{R}\;\text{and }\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{N})

(D-SDP
{}_{n}^{m,\leq}
)

\displaystyle\text{minimising }y

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\forall k\neq n\in\mathbb{N},\;y\geq\mu_{k}\\
y\geq 1+\mu_{n}\\
\forall g\in\mathcal{R}_{m,+}(\mathbb{R}_{+}),\;\langle f_{\bm{\mu}},g\rangle\geq 0,\end{cases}

where
f_{\bm{\mu}}=\sum_{k}\mu_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k}
.

This reformulation uses Stieltjes characterisation of non-negative polynomials over
\mathbb{R}_{+}

[
65
]
and is detailed in Appendix
E
.

The second result provides a nontrivial analytical solution to the primal program

(

SDP

m
,
≤

n

)

:
let us define
\bm{F}^{n}=(F_{k}^{n})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
by

\bullet
if
n
is even:

F_{k}^{n}:=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2^{n}}\binom{k}{\frac{k}{2}}\binom{n-k}{\frac{n-k}{2}}&\text{when }k\leq n,k\text{ even},\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

(58)

\bullet
if
n
is odd:

F_{k}^{n}:=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2^{n}}\frac{\binom{n}{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}\binom{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}{\lfloor\frac{k}{2}\rfloor}^{2}}{\binom{n}{k}},&\text{when }k\leq n,\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(59)

In both cases,

\displaystyle F_{n}^{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\binom{n}{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}

(60)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{n+1}

where we used
\binom{n}{j}\leq\binom{n}{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}
for all
j\in\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket
, summed over
j
from 0 to
n
.

Lemma 7
.

For all
m\geq n
,
\bm{F}^{n}
is a feasible solution of

(

SDP

m
,
≤

n

)

. Moreover, it is optimal when
m=n
.

The proof of feasibility consists in checking that the constraints of

(

SDP

m
,
≤

n

)

are satisfied by
\bm{F}^{n}
. To do so, we make use of Zeilberger’s algorithm
[
70
]
, a powerful algorithm for proving binomial identities. The proof of optimality for
m=n
is obtained by deriving an analytical feasible solution of (D-SDP
{}_{n}^{n,\leq}
) with the same optimal value. Note that the optimality of this solution does not play a role in the proof of convergence. We refer to Appendix
F
for a detailed proof.

As a consequence, we obtain the following analytical lower bound for the optimal value of
(LP

n

L
2

)
:

\omega_{n}\geq\omega_{n}^{n,\leq}=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\binom{n}{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}\underset{n\rightarrow+\infty}{\sim}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi n}},

(61)

which is superseded by numerical bounds when
n\geq 3
(see Table
1
).

We now combine Lemma
6
and Lemma
7
to prove Theorem
5
.

Proof.

The feasible set of
(D-SDP

n

m
,
≤

)
is non-empty, by considering the null sequence, which achieves value
1
. Without loss of generality, we add the constraint
y\leq 1
in
(D-LP

n

S

)
and
(D-SDP

n

m
,
≤

)
.

Let
m\geq n
and let
(y,\bm{\mu})\in\mathbb{R}\times S^{\prime}(\mathbb{N})
be a feasible solution of
(D-SDP

n

m
,
≤

)
expressed in the form given by Lemma
6
.
The constraint
\braket{f_{\bm{\mu}},x\mapsto e^{-\frac{x}{2}}}\geq 0
implies
\mu_{0}\geq 0
and thus
y\geq 0
. Without loss of generality, we may set
\mu_{k}=0
for
k>m
, since these coefficients are only constrained by
\mu_{k}\leq y\leq 1
. We also have
\mu_{k}\leq 1
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

By Lemma
7
,
\bm{F}^{l}
is a feasible solution of (SDP
{}_{l}^{m,\leq}
) for all
l\leq m
, so in particular
f_{\bm{F}^{l}}=\sum_{k=0}^{l}F_{k}^{l}\mathcal{L}_{k}\in\mathcal{R}_{m,+}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
. Hence,
\bm{\mu}
satisfies the constraint
\braket{f_{\bm{\mu}},f_{\bm{F}^{l}}}\geq 0
, which gives

\sum_{k=0}^{l}\mu_{k}F_{k}^{l}\geq 0,

(62)

for all
l\leq m
.
Thus we have, for all
l\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
,

\displaystyle\mu_{l}

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{1}{F_{l}^{l}}\sum_{k=0}^{l-1}\mu_{k}F_{k}^{l}

(63)

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{1}{F_{l}^{l}}\sum_{k=0}^{l-1}F_{k}^{l}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{1}{F_{l}^{l}}

\displaystyle\geq-l,

where we used
F_{l}^{l}>0
in the first line,
\mu_{k}\leq 1
and
F_{k}^{l}\geq 0
in the second line,
\sum_{k=0}^{l}F_{k}^{l}=1
in the third line and Eq. (
60
) in the last line. With
\mu_{k}\leq 1
, we obtain
|\mu_{k}|\leq k
for
k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, and thus
|\mu_{k}|\leq k+1
6
6

6

Critically, this bound does not depend on the level
m
of the hierarchy.
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Hence, the feasible set of
(D-SDP

n

m
,
≤

)
is compact and the program
(D-SDP

n

m
,
≤

)
has feasible optimal solutions for all
m\geq n
, by diagonal extraction.

Let
(y^{m},\bm{\mu}^{m})_{m\geq n}
be a sequence of optimal solutions of
(D-SDP

n

m
,
≤

)
, for
m\geq n
. By Theorem
2
, we have strong duality between the programs

(

SDP

m
,
≤

n

)

and
(D-SDP

n

m
,
≤

)
, so the optimal value of
(D-SDP

n

m
,
≤

)
is given by
\omega_{n}^{m,\leq}
, for all
m\geq n
. By optimality
y^{m}=\omega_{n}^{m,\leq}
, for all
m\geq n
, and the sequence
(y^{m})_{m\geq n}
converges.

Performing a diagonal extraction
\phi
on the sequence
(\bm{\mu}^{m})_{m\geq n}
, we obtain a sequence of sequences
(\bm{\mu}^{\phi(m)})_{m\geq n}
such that each sequence
(\mu^{\phi(m)}_{k})_{m\geq n}
converges when
m\rightarrow+\infty
, for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\mu_{k}
denote its limit, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
. We write
\bm{\mu}=(\mu_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
the sequence of limits. We also write

y:=\lim_{m\rightarrow+\infty}y^{m}=\lim_{m\rightarrow+\infty}\omega_{n}^{m,\leq}.

(64)

For all
m\geq n
, we have
\omega_{n}^{\phi(m),\leq}\geq\mu_{k}^{\phi(m)}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\omega_{n}^{\phi(m),\leq}\geq 1+\mu_{n}^{\phi(m)}
, so taking
m\rightarrow+\infty
we obtain
y\geq\mu_{k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
y\geq 1+\mu_{n}
. By Eq. (
57
), we have
\omega^{m,\leq}_{n}\leq\omega_{n}^{\mathcal{S}}
for all
m\geq n
, so
y\leq\omega_{n}^{\mathcal{S}}
.

Moreover,
|\mu_{k}^{\phi(m)}|\leq k+1
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, so taking
m\rightarrow+\infty
we obtain
|\mu_{k}|\leq k+1
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, which implies that
\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{N})

[
64
]
. Let
f_{\bm{\mu}}=\sum_{k}\mu_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
. We have

\mu_{k}=\braket{f_{\bm{\mu}},\mathcal{L}_{k}}.

(65)

By construction we also have:

\forall m\geq n,\forall g\in\mathcal{R}_{m,+}(\mathbb{R}_{+}),\;\braket{f_{\bm{\mu}},g}\geq 0.

(66)

By Theorem
1
, this implies that the distribution
\mu:=f_{\bm{\mu}}(x)\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
is non-negative, i.e.,

\forall f\in\mathcal{S}_{+}(\mathbb{R}_{+}),\;\braket{f_{\bm{\mu}},f}\geq 0.

(67)

With the constraints
y\geq\mu_{k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
y\geq 1+\mu_{n}
, we have that
(y,\mu)
is a feasible solution of
(D-LP

n

S

)
, and in particular
y\geq{\omega_{n}^{\prime\mathcal{S}}}
, since
(D-LP

n

S

)
is a minimisation problem. Since
y\leq\omega_{n}^{\mathcal{S}}
we obtain with Eq. (
57
) and Eq. (
64
):

\lim_{m\rightarrow+\infty}\omega^{m,\leq}_{n}=\omega_{n}^{\mathcal{S}}={\omega_{n}^{\prime\mathcal{S}}}.

(68)

∎

As a direct corollary of the proofs of Theorem
4
and Theorem

(in the same spirit than the remark after the proof of Theorem

), we obtain the following strong duality result:

Theorem 6
.

Strong duality holds between the programs
(LP

)
and
(D-LP

n

)
and between the programs
(LP

)
and
(D-LP

)
.

For completeness, we give a different and more direct proof of the strong duality between
(LP

)
and
(D-LP

)
in Appendix

.

We have shown the convergence of the semidefinite hierarchies of upper and lower bounds:
(\omega^{m,\geq}_{n})_{m\geq n}
towards the optimal value of
(LP

n

L
2

)
and

(\omega^{m,\leq}_{n})_{m\geq n}
towards the optimal value of
(LP

n

S

)
.
By linearity, these results generalise straightforwardly to the case of witnesses corresponding to linear combinations of fidelities with displaced Fock states:

\displaystyle\lim_{m\rightarrow+\infty}\!\omega^{m,\leq}_{\bm{a}}=\omega_{\bm{a}}^{\mathcal{S}},

(69)

\displaystyle\lim_{m\rightarrow+\infty}\!\omega^{m,\geq}_{\bm{a}}=\omega_{\bm{a}}^{L^{2}},

(70)

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and all
\bm{a}\in[0,1]^{n}
.

We obtained two hierarchies providing numerical lower bounds and upper bounds on the threshold value. A natural question that arises is the following: is there a gap between the optimal values of
(LP

n

L
2

)
and
(LP

n

S

)
? We leave this as an open question.

6
Witnessing multimode Wigner negativity

In this section we discuss the generalisation of our Wigner negativity witnesses to the more challenging multimode setting. Hereafter,
M
denotes the number of modes.

6.1
Multimode Wigner negativity witnesses

Using multi-index notations (see Appendix
H.1
), the single-mode Wigner negativity witnesses defined in Eq. (
6
) are naturally generalised to

\hat{\Omega}_{\bm{a},\bm{\alpha}}:=\sum_{\bm{1}\leq\bm{k}\leq\bm{n}}a_{\bm{k}}\hat{D}(\bm{\alpha})\ket{\bm{k}}\!\bra{\bm{k}}\hat{D}^{\dagger}(\bm{\alpha}),

(71)

for
\bm{n}=(n_{1},\dots,n_{M})\in\mathbb{N}^{M}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
,
\bm{a}=(a_{\bm{k}})_{\bm{1}\leq\bm{k}\leq\bm{n}}\in[0,1]^{n_{1}\cdots n_{M}}
,
with
\max_{\bm{k}}a_{\bm{k}}=1
, and
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{C}^{M}
. Similar to the single-mode case, these POVM elements are weighted sums of multimode displaced Fock states projectors, and their expectation value for a quantum state
\bm{\rho}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}^{\otimes M})
is given by

\text{Tr}(\hat{\Omega}_{\bm{a},\bm{\alpha}}\bm{\rho})=\sum_{\bm{1}\leq\bm{k}\leq\bm{n}}a_{\bm{k}}F\left(\hat{D}^{\dagger}(\bm{\alpha})\bm{\rho}\hat{D}(\bm{\alpha}),\ket{\bm{k}}\right)

(72)

where
F
is the fidelity. Unlike in the single-mode case however, estimating this quantity with homodyne or heterodyne detection by direct fidelity estimation is no longer efficient when the number of modes becomes large. Instead, one may use robust lower bounds on the multimode fidelity from
[
44
]
which can be obtained efficiently with homodyne or heterodyne detection. A lower bound on the estimated experimental multimode fidelity will allow to detect Wigner negativity if it is larger than an upper bound on the threshold value associated to a given witness.

These lower bounds are obtained as follows: given a target multimode Fock state
\ket{\bm{n}}=\ket{n_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\ket{n_{M}}
and multiple copies of an
M
-mode experimental state
\bm{\rho}
, measure all single-mode subsystems of
\bm{\rho}
and perform fidelity estimation with each corresponding single-mode target Fock state. That is, the samples obtained from the detection of the
i^{th}
mode of
\bm{\rho}
are used for single-mode fidelity estimation with the Fock state
\ket{n_{i}}
. Let
F_{1},\dots,F_{M}
be the single-mode fidelity estimates obtained and let
\tilde{F}(\bm{\rho},\ket{\bm{n}}):=1-\sum_{i=1}^{M}(1-F_{i})
. Then
[
44
]
,

\hskip-4.26773pt1\!-\!M(1\!-\!F(\bm{\rho},\ket{\bm{n}}))\leq\tilde{F}(\bm{\rho},\ket{\bm{n}})\leq F(\bm{\rho},\ket{\bm{n}}).

(73)

In particular,
\tilde{F}
provides a good estimate of the multimode fidelity
F
whenever
F
is not too small.

The same procedure is followed in the case of target displaced Fock states, with classical translations of the samples in order to account for the displacement parameters.

To each witness
\hat{\Omega}_{\bm{a},\bm{\alpha}}
is associated its threshold value:

\omega_{\bm{a}}:=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{\rho}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}^{\otimes M})\\
W_{\bm{\rho}}\geq 0\end{subarray}}\text{Tr}\!\left(\hat{\Omega}_{\bm{a},\bm{\alpha}}\,\bm{\rho}\right).

(74)

With Eq. (
73
), if the value of the bound
\tilde{F}
obtained experimentally is greater than
\omega_{\bm{a}}
, then the state
\bm{\rho}
has a negative Wigner function.

With the same arguments as in the single-mode case, the multimode Wigner negativity witnesses in Eq. (
71
) form a complete family and retain the interpretation from Lemma
1
: the violation of the threshold value provides a lower bound on the distance to the set of multimode states with non-negative Wigner function. However, the limited robustness of the bound
\tilde{F}
may affect the performance of the witnesses in practical scenarios, in particular for witnesses that are sums of different projectors. Still, we show in section
6.3
the applicability of the method with a genuinely multimode example.

We first generalise the single-mode semidefinite programming approach for approximating the threshold values to the multimode case.

6.2
Approximating the multimode threshold values

By linearity, we restrict our analysis to the case of Wigner negativity witnesses that are projectors onto a single multimode Fock state
\ket{\bm{n}}
, for
\bm{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{M}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
. We thus consider the computation of

\omega_{\bm{n}}=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{\rho}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}^{\otimes M})\\
W_{\bm{\rho}}\geq 0\end{subarray}}\braket{\bm{n}|\bm{\rho}|\bm{n}}.

(75)

A similar reasoning to the single-mode case shows that the computation of the corresponding threshold value in Eq. (
74
) may be rephrased as the following infinite-dimensional linear program:

\displaystyle\text{Find }(F_{\bm{k}})_{\bm{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{M}}\in\ell^{2}

(LP
{}_{\bm{n}}^{L^{2}}
)

\displaystyle\text{maximising }F_{\bm{n}}

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\sum_{\bm{k}}F_{\bm{k}}=1\\
\forall\bm{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{M},\;F_{\bm{k}}\geq 0\\
\forall\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{M},\;\sum_{\bm{k}}F_{\bm{k}}\mathcal{L}_{\bm{k}}(\bm{x})\geq 0,\end{cases}

where the optimisation is over square-summable real sequences indexed by elements of
\mathbb{N}^{M}
. Its dual linear program reads

\displaystyle\text{Find }y\in\mathbb{R}\text{ and }\mu\in{L^{2}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{M})

(D-LP
{}_{\bm{n}}^{L^{2}}
)

\displaystyle\text{minimising }y

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\forall\bm{k}\neq\bm{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{M},\;y\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{M}}{\mathcal{L}_{\bm{k}}}{d\mu}\\
y\geq 1+\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{M}}{\mathcal{L}_{\bm{n}}}{d\mu}\\
\forall f\in L^{2}_{+}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{M}),\;\langle\mu,f\rangle\geq 0.\end{cases}

We denote their optimal value
\omega_{\bm{n}}^{L^{2}}
—as in the single-mode case, we have strong duality between these programs.
We also introduce the programs over Schwartz functions:

\displaystyle\text{Find }(F_{\bm{k}})_{\bm{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{M}}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{N}^{M})

(LP
{}_{\bm{n}}^{\mathcal{S}}
)

\displaystyle\text{maximising }F_{\bm{n}}

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\sum_{\bm{k}}F_{\bm{k}}=1\\
\forall\bm{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{M},\;F_{\bm{k}}\geq 0\\
\forall\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{M},\;\sum_{\bm{k}}F_{\bm{k}}\mathcal{L}_{\bm{k}}(\bm{x})\geq 0,\end{cases}

and

\displaystyle\text{Find }y\in\mathbb{R}\text{ and }\mu\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{M})

(D-LP
{}_{\bm{n}}^{\mathcal{S}}
)

\displaystyle\text{minimising }y

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\forall\bm{k}\neq\bm{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{M},\;y\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{M}}{\mathcal{L}_{\bm{k}}}{d\mu}\\
y\geq 1+\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{M}}{\mathcal{L}_{\bm{n}}}{d\mu}\\
\forall f\in L^{2}_{+}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{M}),\;\langle\mu,f\rangle\geq 0.\end{cases}

We denote their optimal value
\omega_{\bm{n}}^{S}
.

In the single-mode case, we obtained hierarchies of SDP relaxations and restrictions for
(LP

n

L
2

)
by replacing constraints involving non-negative functions by constraints involving non-negative polynomials
P
of fixed degree.
We then exploited the existence of a sum-of-squares decomposition for non-negative monovariate polynomials.
In the multimode setting, the polynomials involved are multivariate, so that the set of non-negative polynomials over
\mathbb{R}
of a given degree may be strictly larger than the set of sum-of-square polynomials
[
68
]
. Instead, we replace directly constraints involving non-negative functions over
\mathbb{R}_{+}
by constraints involving non-negative polynomials
P
of fixed degree such that
\bm{x}\mapsto P(\bm{x}^{2})
8
8

8

We write
\bm{x}^{2}
in short for
\bm{x}^{2\bf 1}=(x_{1}^{2},\dots,x_{M}^{2})
.
has a sum-of-squares decomposition, implying that the multimode semidefinite relaxations and restrictions are possibly looser than their single-mode counterparts.

Moreover, the dimension of the semidefinite programs increases exponentially with the level of the hierarchy
m
, as the number of
M
-variate monomials of degree less or equal to
m
is given by
\binom{M+m}{m}
. This implies that the semidefinite programs remain tractable only for a constant number of levels.

In spite of these observations, and following similar steps to the single-mode case (see Appendix
H.2
), the SDP relaxations providing upper bounds for the threshold value are given by

\displaystyle\text{Find }A=(A_{\bm{i}\bm{j}})_{|\bm{i}|,|\bm{j}|\leq m}\in\text{Sym}_{\binom{M+m}{m}}\hskip-85.35826pt

(\text{SDP}^{m,\geq}_{\bm{n}})

\displaystyle\text{and }\bm{F}=(F_{\bm{k}})_{|\bm{k}|\leq m}\in\mathbb{R}^{{\binom{M+m}{m}}}\hskip-113.81102pt

\displaystyle\text{maximising }F_{\bm{n}}

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\textstyle\sum_{|\bm{k}|\leq m}F_{\bm{k}}=1\\
\textstyle\forall|\bm{k}|\leq m,\;F_{\bm{k}}\geq 0\\
\textstyle\forall|\bm{l}|\leq m,\forall\bm{i}\!+\!\bm{j}\!=\!2\bm{l},\;A_{\bm{i}\bm{j}}=\sum\limits_{\bm{k}\leq\bm{l}}F_{\bm{k}}\binom{\bm{l}}{\bm{k}}\bm{l}!\hskip-85.35826pt\\
\textstyle\forall|\bm{r}|\leq 2m,\bm{r}\!\neq\!2\bm{l},\forall|\bm{l}|\leq m,\forall\bm{i}\!+\!\bm{j}\!=\!\bm{r},\;A_{\bm{i}\bm{j}}=0\hskip-85.35826pt\\
\textstyle A\succeq 0,\end{cases}

for all
m\geq|\bm{n}|
.
Similarly, the semidefinite restrictions providing lower bounds for the threshold value are given by

\displaystyle\text{Find }Q=(Q_{\bm{i}\bm{j}})_{|\bm{i}|,|\bm{j}|\leq m}\in\text{Sym}_{\binom{M+m}{m}}\hskip-113.81102pt

(\text{SDP}^{m,\leq}_{\bm{n}})

\displaystyle\text{and }\bm{F}=(F_{\bm{k}})_{|\bm{k}|\leq m}\in\mathbb{R}^{{\binom{M+m}{m}}}\hskip-113.81102pt

\displaystyle\text{maximising }F_{\bm{n}}

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\textstyle\sum_{|\bm{k}|\leq m}F_{\bm{k}}=1\\
\textstyle\forall|\bm{k}|\leq m,\;F_{\bm{k}}\geq 0\\
\textstyle\forall|\bm{l}|\leq m,\sum_{\bm{i}+\bm{j}=2\bm{l}}Q_{\bm{i}\bm{j}}=\sum_{\bm{k}\geq\bm{l}}\frac{(-1)^{|\bm{k}|+|\bm{l}|}}{\bm{l}!}\binom{\bm{k}}{\bm{l}}F_{\bm{k}}\hskip-113.81102pt\\
\textstyle\forall|\bm{r}|\leq 2m,\bm{r}\neq 2\bm{l},\forall|\bm{l}|\leq m,\sum_{\bm{i}+\bm{j}=\bm{r}}Q_{\bm{i}\bm{j}}=0\hskip-113.81102pt\\
\textstyle Q\succeq 0,\end{cases}

for all
m\geq|\bm{n}|
. In these semidefinite programs, the optimisations are over matrices and vectors indexed by elements of
\mathbb{N}^{m}
with sum of coefficients lower that
m
.

While our proof of convergence of the single-mode hierarchy of upper bounds towards
\omega_{\bm{n}}^{L^{2}}
transfers easily to the multimode setting, the proof of convergence of the hierarchy of lower bounds towards
\omega_{\bm{n}}^{\mathcal{S}}
requires the analytical expression of feasible solutions for each level of the hierarchy. We show how to construct such solutions in the multimode case using products of single-mode feasible solutions—this requires introducing an equivalent hierarchy of restrictions, where constraints are expressed on polynomials of
M
variables with the degree in each individual variable being less or equal to
m
, rather than on polynomials of degree
m
(that is, constraints of the form
\bm{k}\leq\bm{m}
where
\bm{m}=(m,\dots,m)\in\mathbb{N}^{M}
rather than
|\bm{k}|\leq m
). Along the way, we also prove strong duality of the programs involved. We refer to Appendix
H.3
for the proofs.

Summarising our results, we find that the multimode semidefinite programs

(

SDP

m
,
≥

n

)

and

(

SDP

m
,
≤

n

)

provide converging sequences of upper and lower bounds to the threshold values
\omega_{\bm{n}}^{L^{2}}
and
\omega_{\bm{n}}^{\mathcal{S}}
, respectively.
We study a concrete application in the next section.

6.3
Multimode example

To illustrate the usefulness of our Wigner negativity witnesses in the multimode setting, we consider a lossy Fock state over two modes:

\displaystyle\bm{\rho}_{1,1,\eta}

\displaystyle:=(1-\eta)^{2}\ket{1}\!\bra{1}\otimes\ket{1}\!\bra{1}

(76)

\displaystyle+\eta(1-\eta)\ket{1}\!\bra{1}\otimes\ket{0}\!\bra{0}

\displaystyle+\eta(1-\eta)\ket{0}\!\bra{0}\otimes\ket{1}\!\bra{1}

\displaystyle+\eta^{2}\ket{0}\!\bra{0}\otimes\ket{0}\!\bra{0},

with loss parameter
0\leq\eta\leq 1
. Setting
\eta=0
gives
\bm{\rho}_{1,1,\eta}=\ket{1}\!\bra{1}\otimes\ket{1}\!\bra{1}
while setting
\eta=1
gives
\bm{\rho}_{1,1,\eta}=\ket{0}\!\bra{0}\otimes\ket{0}\!\bra{0}
. This state has a non-negative Wigner function for
\eta\geq\frac{1}{2}
.

We also consider the multimode Wigner negativity witness
\ket{1}\!\bra{1}\otimes\ket{1}\!\bra{1}
, which is a projector onto the Fock state
\ket{1}\otimes\ket{1}
. Solving numerically the corresponding hierarchy

(

SDP

m
,
≤

n

)

up to
m=3
, we obtain the lower bound
0.266
and solving the hierarchy

(

SDP

m
,
≥

n

)

up to
m=10
, we obtain the upper bound
0.320
.

A direct consequence of the numerical lower bound is that tensor product states are not the closest among Wigner positive states to tensor product states with a negative Wigner function. Indeed, the maximum achievable fidelity with the state
\ket{1}\otimes\ket{1}
using Wigner positive tensor product states is equal to the square of the maximum achievable fidelity with the state
\ket{1}
using single-mode Wigner positive states, that is
0.5^{2}=0.25<0.266
.

Figure 5:
Witnessing Wigner negativity of the lossy Fock state
\bm{\rho}_{1,1,\eta}
over two modes using the witness
\ket{1}\!\bra{1}\otimes\ket{1}\!\bra{1}
. The threshold value for that witness is upper bounded by
0.320
and lower bounded by
0.266
. The dashed red line delimits the interval of loss parameter values where the witness can be used to detect Wigner negativity of
\bm{\rho}_{1,1,\eta}
efficiently, i.e., when the robust bound
\tilde{F}(\bm{\rho}_{1,1,\eta},\ket{1}\otimes\ket{1})
(blue curve) on the fidelity from Eq. (
78
) is above the witness upper bound (red line). When it is below the witness lower bound (black line), we are guaranteed that the witness cannot be used to detect Wigner negativity of the state. The fidelity
F(\bm{\rho}_{1,1,\eta},\ket{1}\otimes\ket{1})
is also depicted above (yellow curve). Note that
\bm{\rho}_{1,1,\eta}
has a non-negative Wigner function for
\eta\geq 0.5
.

We now use the upper bound to witness the Wigner negativity of the state
\bm{\rho}_{1,1\eta}
(see Fig.
5
).
The fidelity between
\bm{\rho}_{1,1,\eta}
and
\ket{1}\otimes\ket{1}
is given by
F(\bm{\rho}_{1,1,\eta},\ket{1}\otimes\ket{1})=(1-\eta)^{2}
, for all
0\leq\eta\leq 1
. This fidelity is above the upper bound
0.320
on the threshold value of the witness
\ket{1}\!\bra{1}\otimes\ket{1}\!\bra{1}
when
\eta\leq 0.434
.

However, in practice one would not obtain a precise estimate of the fidelity efficiently, but rather a robust lower bound on the fidelity computed from single-mode fidelities, which satisfies Eq. (
73
). In the worst case, the estimate obtained is closer to
1-2(1-F(\bm{\rho}_{1,1,\eta},\ket{1}\otimes\ket{1}))
than to
F(\bm{\rho}_{1,1,\eta},\ket{1}\otimes\ket{1})
. When the value of this robust lower bound is greater than the threshold value of the witness, this implies that the state has a negative Wigner function.

In the present case, the two single-mode reduced states of
\bm{\rho}_{1,1,\eta}
are the same, given by

\text{Tr}_{2}(\bm{\rho}_{1,1,\eta})=(1-\eta)\ket{1}\!\bra{1}+\eta\ket{0}\!\bra{0},

(77)

so the single-mode fidelities with
\ket{1}
are equal for each mode and given by
1-\eta
. Hence, the robust lower bound on
F(\bm{\rho}_{1,1,\eta},\ket{1}\otimes\ket{1})
is given by

\tilde{F}(\bm{\rho}_{1,1,\eta},\ket{1}\otimes\ket{1})=1-2\eta.

(78)

It is above the upper bound
0.320
on the threshold value of the witness
\ket{1}\!\bra{1}\otimes\ket{1}\!\bra{1}
when
\eta\leq 0.340
.

This example highlights the use of efficient and robust lower bounds on multimode fidelities rather than fidelity estimates
[
44
]
, in conjunction with our family of multimode witnesses to detect Wigner negativity of realistic experimental states.

7
Conclusion and open problems

Characterising quantum properties of physical systems is an important step in the development of quantum technologies, and negativity of the Wigner function, a necessary resource for any quantum computational speedup, is no exception.
In this work, we have derived a complete family of Wigner negativity witnesses which provide an operational quantification of Wigner negativity, both in the single-mode and multimode settings.
In the context of quantum optical information processing, the main application of our method is in experimental scenarios, where it leads to robust and efficient certification of negativity of the Wigner function. Witnesses of Wigner negativity also provide witnesses of non-classicality although they are no longer complete in this case.

What is more, our witnesses also delineate the set of quantum states with positive Wigner function, and it would be interesting to understand whether additional insights on this set can be obtained using these witnesses.

The Wigner function has been extended to the discrete-variable setting
[
71
,
72
]
, where it has been been linked to contextuality
[
73
,
74
,
75
]
, a necessary resource for discrete-variable quantum computing
[
76
,
77
]
. A framework for treating contextuality and computing the amount of contextuality in continuous-variable settings has recently been developed
[
78
]
. As we obtained reliable Wigner negativity witnesses, it would be interesting to investigate the link between Wigner negativity and continuous-variable contextuality.

Hierarchies of semidefinite programs (in particular with non-commutative variables
[
79
]
) have found many recent applications in quantum information theory.
From an infinite-dimensional linear program, we were able to use numerically both a hierarchy of upper bounds and a hierarchy of lower bounds—thus obtaining a certificate for the optimality of these bounds by looking at their difference—whereas this only works in specific cases for the Lasserre hierarchy of upper bounds
[
35
]
. Can we find other interesting cases where we can exploit both hierarchies? Moreover, we obtained an analytical sequence of lower bounds for the threshold value of the program
(LP

n

L
2

)
. Can we also get an analytical sequence of upper bounds? In particular, we anticipate that Fock states
\ket{n}
get further away from the set of states having a positive Wigner function as
n
increases and that
\omega_{n}=\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}})
as
n\rightarrow+\infty
.
The question of the gap between the optimisation over square-integrable functions and Schwartz functions is also left open.

Finally, using our multimode Wigner negativity witnesses for studying the interplay between Wigner negativity and entanglement
[
52
]
is a very interesting prospect which we leave for future work.

Appendix A
Riesz–Haviland theorem in Laguerre basis

Let
\bm{\nu}=(\nu_{l})_{l\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
. Let us introduce the Riesz functional

\displaystyle L_{\bm{\nu}}:\quad\mathbb{R}[x]

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\quad\mathbb{R}

(79)

\displaystyle P(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{p}p_{k}x^{k}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\quad\sum_{k=0}^{p}p_{k}\nu_{k},

which maps real polynomials to real numbers. Let
K
be a closed subset of
\mathbb{R}
. We say that
L_{\bm{\nu}}
is
K
-non-negative if
L_{\bm{\nu}}(P)\geq 0
for all
P\in\mathbb{R}[x]
non-negative on
K
.
We recall the classical Riesz–Haviland theorem
[
66
,
67
]
(see, e.g.,
[
80
]
for a recent formulation):

Theorem 7

(Riesz–Haviland)
.

The sequence
\bm{\nu}=(\nu_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
is the sequence of moments
\int_{K}x^{k}d\nu(x)
of a non-negative distribution
\nu
supported on
K
if and only if
L_{\bm{\nu}}
is
K
-non-negative.

We prove a modified version of this result in the basis of Laguerre functions. To that end, we introduce the following change of basis:

Lemma 8
.

Let
\bm{\mu},\bm{\nu}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
. For all
m\in\mathbb{N}
, the following conditions are equivalent:

(i)

\forall k\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket,\quad\mu_{k}=\sum_{l=0}^{k}\nu_{l}\frac{(-1)^{k+l}}{l!}\binom{k}{l}
,

(ii)

\forall l\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket,\quad\nu_{l}=\sum_{k=0}^{l}\mu_{k}\binom{l}{k}l!
.

As a direct consequence, we retrieve the formula:

x^{l}=\sum_{k=0}^{l}(-1)^{k}\binom{l}{k}l!L_{k}(x),

(80)

for all
l\in\mathbb{N}
and all
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

Proof.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii): suppose that

\forall k\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket,\quad\mu_{k}=\sum_{p=0}^{k}\nu_{p}\frac{(-1)^{k+p}}{p!}\binom{k}{p}.

(81)

Then, for all
l\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket
,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{l}\mu_{k}\binom{l}{k}l!

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{l}\sum_{p=0}^{k}\nu_{p}\frac{(-1)^{k+p}}{p!}\binom{k}{p}\binom{l}{k}l!

(82)

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=0}^{l}\nu_{p}\frac{l!}{p!}\binom{l}{p}\sum_{k=p}^{l}(-1)^{k-p}\binom{l-p}{k-p}

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=0}^{l}\nu_{p}\frac{l!}{p!}\binom{l}{p}\sum_{q=0}^{l-p}(-1)^{q}\binom{l-p}{q}

\displaystyle=\nu_{l},

where we used Eq. (
81
) in the first line and the binomial theorem in the last line which imposes
l=p
.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i): suppose that

\forall l\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket,\quad\nu_{l}=\sum_{p=0}^{l}\mu_{p}\binom{l}{p}l!.

(83)

Then, for all
k\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket
,

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{k}\nu_{l}\frac{(-1)^{k+l}}{l!}\binom{k}{l}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{k}\sum_{p=0}^{l}\mu_{p}(-1)^{k+l}\binom{l}{p}\binom{k}{l}

(84)

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=0}^{k}\mu_{p}(-1)^{k+p}\binom{k}{p}\sum_{l=p}^{k}(-1)^{l-p}\binom{k-p}{l-p}

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=0}^{k}\mu_{p}(-1)^{k+p}\binom{k}{p}\sum_{q=0}^{k-p}(-1)^{q}\binom{k-p}{q}

\displaystyle=\mu_{k},

where we used Eq. (
83
) in the first line and the binomial theorem in the last line which imposes
k=p
.

∎

We may now prove the Riesz–Haviland theorem in Laguerre basis (Theorem
1
from the main text):

Theorem 1
.

Let
\bm{\mu}=(\mu_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
. The sequence
\bm{\mu}
is the sequence of Laguerre moments
\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\mathcal{L}_{k}(x)d\mu(x)
of a non-negative distribution
\mu
supported on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
if and only if

\forall m\in\mathbb{N},\forall g\in\mathcal{R}_{m,+}(\mathbb{R}_{+}),\;\braket{f_{\bm{\mu}},g}\geq 0.

(85)

Proof.

Let
\bm{\mu}=(\mu_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
, and suppose that the sequence
\bm{\mu}
is the sequence of Laguerre moments
\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\mathcal{L}_{k}(x)d\mu(x)
of a non-negative distribution
\mu
supported on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

Let
m\geq 0
and let
g=\sum_{k=0}^{m}g_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k}\in\mathcal{R}_{m,+}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
. The distribution
\mu
is non-negative, so
\braket{\mu,g}\geq 0
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\braket{f_{\bm{\mu}},g}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m}{\mu_{k}g_{k}}

(86)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\sum_{k=0}^{m}{g_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k}d\mu}

\displaystyle=\braket{\mu,g}.

Hence, for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
and all
g\in\mathcal{R}_{m,+}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
,
\braket{f_{\bm{\mu}},g}\geq 0
.

Conversely, let
\bm{\mu}=(\mu_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
, and suppose that for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
and all
g\in\mathcal{R}_{m,+}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
,
\braket{f_{\bm{\mu}},g}\geq 0
.
We define the sequence
\bm{\nu}=(\nu_{l})_{l\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
by

\nu_{l}:=\sum_{k=0}^{l}\mu_{k}\binom{l}{k}l!,

(87)

for all
l\in\mathbb{N}
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
and let
P(x)=\sum_{l=0}^{m}p_{l}x^{l}
be a non-negative polynomial over
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. By Eq. (
80
), for all
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

\displaystyle P(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{m}p_{l}\sum_{k=0}^{l}(-1)^{k}\binom{l}{k}l!L_{k}(x)

(88)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m}(-1)^{k}L_{k}(x)\left(\sum_{l=k}^{m}p_{l}\binom{l}{k}l!\right).

Let
g_{P}(x):=P(x)e^{-\frac{x}{2}}
, for
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. We have
g_{P}\in\mathcal{R}_{m,+}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, so
\braket{f_{\bm{\mu}},g_{P}}\geq 0
. Moreover, with Eq. (
88
)

\displaystyle\braket{f_{\bm{\mu}},g_{P}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m}\mu_{k}\left(\sum_{l=k}^{m}p_{l}\binom{l}{k}l!\right)

(89)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{m}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{l}\mu_{k}\binom{l}{k}l!\right)p_{l}

\displaystyle=L_{\bm{\nu}}(P)

where we used Eq. (
87
) and the definition of the Riesz functional from Eq. (
79
) in the last line.
In particular,
L_{\bm{\nu}}(P)\geq 0
, and this holds for all non-negative polynomials
P
over
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. By the Riesz–Haviland theorem (Theorem
7
), this implies that
\bm{\nu}
is the sequence of moments of a non-negative distribution
\nu
supported on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

Furthermore, we have that for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
:

\displaystyle\mu_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{k}\nu_{l}\frac{(-1)^{k+l}}{l!}\binom{k}{l}

(90)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{k}\frac{(-1)^{k+l}}{l!}\binom{k}{l}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}x^{l}d\nu(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(-1)^{k}\sum_{l=0}^{k}\frac{(-1)^{l}}{l!}\binom{k}{l}x^{l}d\nu(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(-1)^{k}L_{k}(x)d\nu(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\mathcal{L}_{k}(x)e^{\frac{x}{2}}d\nu(x)

where we used Lemma
8
in the first line. Hence,
\bm{\mu}
is the sequence of Laguerre moments of the distribution
\mu(x):=e^{\frac{x}{2}}\nu(x)
supported on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, which is non-negative since
\nu
is non-negative.

∎

Appendix B
Theory for infinite-dimensional linear programs

This appendix is dedicated to expressing formally our linear program as presented in
[
61
, IV–(6.1)]
so that readers unfamiliar with global optimisation may better understand why
(LP

n

L
2

)
is indeed an infinite-dimensional linear program and how to derive its dual program.
We will derive it for the problem over square-integrable functions. The exact same treatment can be applied for the case of optimising over Schwartz functions which will involve
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and its dual space
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, the space of tempered distributions.
We recall our initial program
(LP

n

L
2

)
:

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\left\{\begin{aligned} &&&\underset{(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}}{\text{sup}}F_{n}\\
&\text{subject to}&&\sum_{k}F_{k}=1\\
&\text{and}&&\forall k\in\mathbb{N},\quad F_{k}\geq 0\\
&\text{and}&&\forall x\in\mathbb{R}_{+},\quad\sum_{k}F_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k}(x)\geq 0.\end{aligned}\right.

(LP
{}_{n}^{L^{2}}
)

Let us introduce the spaces:

•

E_{1}=\ell^{2}\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
9
9

9

Recall that via expansion on a basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
,
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and
\ell^{2}
are isomomorphic and that the family of Laguerre functions forms a basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
.
.

•

F_{1}=\ell^{2}\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
10
10

10

The spaces
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and
{L^{2}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
are isomomorphic by the Radon–Nikodym theorem. We associate a measure in the dual of
{L^{2}}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
with the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
times the corresponding function in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
.
the dual space of
E_{1}
.

•

E_{2}=\mathbb{R}\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
.

•

F_{2}=\mathbb{R}\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
the dual space of
E_{2}
.

We also define the dualities
\langle\text{--},\text{--}\rangle_{1}:E_{1}\times F_{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
\langle\text{--},\text{--}\rangle_{2}:E_{2}\times F_{2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
as follows:

\displaystyle\forall e_{1}=((u_{k}),f)\in E_{1},\forall f_{1}=((v_{k}),\mu)\in F_{1},

\displaystyle\langle e_{1},f_{1}\rangle_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{k}u_{k}v_{k}+\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}{f}{d\mu},

(91)

\displaystyle\forall e_{2}=(x,f)\in E_{2},\forall f_{2}=(y,\mu)\in F_{2},

\displaystyle\langle e_{2},f_{2}\rangle_{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}xy+\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}{f}{d\mu}.

Let
A\colon E_{1}\longrightarrow E_{2}
be the following linear transformation:

\forall e_{1}=((u_{k}),f)\in E_{1},\quad A(e_{1})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left(\sum_{k}u_{k},x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\mapsto f(x)-\sum_{k}u_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k}(x)\right),

(92)

and
A^{*}\colon F_{2}\longrightarrow F_{1}
be defined as:

\forall f_{2}=(y,\mu)\in F_{2},\quad A^{*}(f_{2})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left((y-\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}{\mathcal{L}_{k}}{d\mu})_{k\in\mathbb{N}},\mu\right).

(93)

We can easily verify that
A^{*}
is the dual transformation of
A
, i.e.,
\forall e_{1}\in E_{1},\forall f_{2}\in F_{2}
we have
\langle A(e_{1}),f_{2}\rangle_{2}=\langle e_{1},A^{*}(f_{2})\rangle_{1}
.

Recall that
L^{2}_{+}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
is the cone of non-negative functions in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and
\ell^{2}_{+}
the cone of sequences in
\ell^{2}
with non-negative coefficients.
We will optimise in the convex cones
K_{1}=\ell^{2}_{+}\times L^{2}_{+}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\subset E_{1}
and
K_{2}=\{0\}
. The dual cones are then respectively:
K_{1}^{*}=\{f_{1}\in F_{1}:\forall e_{1}\in K_{1},\;\langle e_{1},f_{1}\rangle\geq 0\}
and
K_{2}^{*}=F_{2}
.

We can now rewrite the problem
(LP

n

L
2

)
as a standard linear program in convex cones. We choose the vector function in the objective to be
c_{n}=((\delta_{kn})_{k},\mathbf{0})\in F_{1}
and we also set
b=(1,\mathbf{0})\in E_{2}
for the constraints. The standard form of
(LP

n

L
2

)
in the sense of
[
61
]
can be written as follows:

\displaystyle\hskip 79.6678pt\left\{\begin{aligned} &&&\sup_{e_{1}\in E_{1}}\langle e_{1},c_{n}\rangle_{1}\\
&\text{subject to}&&A(e_{1})=b\\
&\text{and}&&e_{1}\geq_{K_{1}}0.\\
\end{aligned}\right.

(LP
{}_{n}^{L^{2}}
)

The standard form of the dual
(D-LP

n

L
2

)
of problem
(LP

n

L
2

)
can be expressed as follows:

\displaystyle\hskip 79.6678pt\left\{\begin{aligned} &&&\inf_{f_{2}\in F_{2}}\langle b,f_{2}\rangle_{2}\\
&\text{subject to}&&A^{*}(f_{2})\geq_{K_{1}^{*}}c_{n},\\
\end{aligned}\right.

(D-LP
{}_{n}^{L^{2}}
)

which can be expanded as:

\displaystyle\hskip 79.6678pt\left\{\begin{aligned} &&&\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in\mathbb{R}\\
\mu\in{L^{2}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\end{subarray}}{\text{inf}}y\\
&\text{subject to}&&\forall k\in\mathbb{N},\quad y\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}{\mathcal{L}_{k}}{d\mu}\\
&\text{and}&&y\geq 1+\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}{\mathcal{L}_{n}}{d\mu}\\
&\text{and}&&\forall f\in L^{2}_{+}(\mathbb{R}_{+}),\quad\langle\mu,f\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}fd\mu\geq 0.\\
\end{aligned}\right.

(D-LP
{}_{n}^{L^{2}}
)

Note that a similar derivation holds for the more general form where one uses a linear combination of fidelities with Fock states. The displaced Fock states version can be obtained by classical post-processing as detailed in section
2.2
. For
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and some vector
\bm{a}=(a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{n})\in[0,1]^{n}
, the computation of:

\omega_{\bm{a}}:=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})\\
W_{\rho}\geq 0\end{subarray}}\text{Tr}\left(\hat{\Omega}_{\bm{a},0}\rho\right),

(94)

can be expressed as:

\displaystyle\hskip 79.6678pt\left\{\begin{aligned} &&&\underset{(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}}{\text{sup}}\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{k}F_{k}\\
&\text{subject to}&&\sum_{k}F_{k}=1\\
&\text{and}&&\forall k\in\mathbb{N},\quad F_{k}\geq 0\\
&\text{and}&&\forall x\in\mathbb{R}_{+},\quad\sum_{k}F_{k}\mathcal{L}(x)\geq 0.\end{aligned}\right.

(
\text{LP}_{\bm{a}}^{L^{2}}
)

Its dual reads:

\displaystyle\hskip 79.6678pt\left\{\begin{aligned} &&&\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in\mathbb{R}\\
\mu\in{L^{2}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\end{subarray}}{\text{inf}}y\\
&\text{subject to}&&\forall k\leq n\in\mathbb{N},\quad y\geq a_{k}+\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}{\mathcal{L}_{k}}{d\mu}\\
&\text{and}&&\forall k>n\in\mathbb{N},\quad y\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}{\mathcal{L}_{k}}{d\mu}\\
&\text{and}&&\forall f\in L^{2}_{+}(\mathbb{R}_{+}),\quad\langle\mu,f\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}fd\mu\geq 0.\\
\end{aligned}\right.

(
\text{D-LP}_{\bm{a}}^{L^{2}}
)

Appendix C
Proof of technical lemmas

In this section we prove the technical lemmas from section
5.4
. For completeness, we include the proof of Lemma
3
below:

Lemma 3

(
[
68
]
)
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{N}
and let
P
be a univariate polynomial of degree
2p
. Let
X=(1,x,\dots,x^{p})
be the vector of monomials. Then,
P
is non-negative over
\mathbb{R}
if and only if there exists a sum of squares decomposition for
P
, i.e., a real
(p+1)\times(p+1)
positive semidefinite matrix
Q
such that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

P(x)=X^{T}QX.

(95)

Proof.

If for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
P(x)=X^{T}QX
with
X=(1,x,\dots,x^{p})
for
Q
positive semidefinite, then
P
is clearly non-negative over
\mathbb{R}
.

Conversely, suppose the univariate polynomial
P
of degree
2p
is non-negative over
\mathbb{R}
. It can thus be written as a sum of squares of polynomials of degree at most
p
(e.g., by considering the factorisation of
P
and writing each term in the product as a sum of squares, given that its zeros on the real line have even multiplicity and that each complex zero is associated to a conjugate zero with same multiplicity): for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

P(x)=\sum_{i}S_{i}^{2}(x),

(96)

for some real polynomials
S_{i}
of degree at most
p
. Then, for some vectors of coefficients
\bm{s}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p+1}
we have

\displaystyle P(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}(\bm{s}_{i}^{T}X)^{2}

(97)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}(X^{T}\bm{s}_{i})(\bm{s}_{i}^{T}X)

\displaystyle=X^{T}\left(\sum_{i}\bm{s}_{i}\bm{s}_{i}^{T}\right)X.

Setting
Q:=\sum_{i}\bm{s}_{i}\bm{s}_{i}^{T}\succeq 0
completes the proof.
∎

We now turn to the proof of Lemma
4
:

Lemma 4
.

Non-negative polynomials on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
can be written as sums of polynomials of the form
\sum_{l=0}^{p}x^{l}\sum_{i+j=2l}y_{i}y_{j}
, where
p\in\mathbb{N}
and
y_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
, for all
0\leq i\leq p
.

Proof.

Let
P
be a univariate polynomial of degree
p
which is non-negative on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
Writing
X=(1,x,\dots,x^{p})
, the polynomial
x\mapsto P(x^{2})
of degree
2p
is non-negative on
\mathbb{R}
, so by Lemma
3
there exists a real positive semidefinite matrix
Q=(Q_{ij})_{0\leq i,j\leq p}
such that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle P(x^{2})

\displaystyle=X^{T}QX

(98)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{2p}x^{k}\sum_{i+j=k}Q_{ij}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{p}x^{2l}\sum_{i+j=2l}Q_{ij},

where the last line comes from the fact that
x\mapsto P(x^{2})
has no monomial of odd degree. Hence, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

P(x)=\sum_{l=0}^{p}x^{l}\sum_{i+j=2l}Q_{ij}.

(99)

Q
is a real
(p+1)\times(p+1)
positive semidefinite matrix, so via Cholesky decomposition

Q=\sum_{k=0}^{p}\bm{y}^{(k)}\bm{y}^{(k)T},

(100)

where
\bm{y}^{(k)}\in\mathbb{R}^{p+1}
for all
k\in\llbracket 0,p\rrbracket
. We finally obtain, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

\displaystyle P(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{p}x^{l}\sum_{i+j=2l}\sum_{k=0}^{p}\left(\bm{y}^{(k)}\bm{y}^{(k)T}\right)_{ij}

(101)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{p}\left(\sum_{l=0}^{p}x^{l}\sum_{i+j=2l}\bm{y}_{i}^{(k)}\bm{y}_{j}^{(k)}\right).

∎

We recall a few definitions from the main text.
For
\bm{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
, we define the associated formal series of Laguerre functions:

f_{\bm{s}}:=\sum_{k\geq 0}s_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k},

(102)

where for all
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
\mathcal{L}_{k}(x)=(-1)^{k}L_{k}(x)e^{-\frac{x}{2}}
, with
L_{k}(x)=\sum_{l=0}^{k}\frac{(-1)^{l}}{l!}\binom{k}{l}x^{l}
the
k^{th}
Laguerre polynomial. For
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we also define the associated matrix
A_{\bm{s}}
by

(A_{\bm{s}})_{0\leq i,j\leq m}=\begin{cases}\sum_{k=0}^{l}s_{k}\binom{l}{k}l!&\text{when }i+j=2l,\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(103)

For all
m\in\mathbb{N}
, the set of series of Laguerre functions over
\mathbb{R}_{+}
truncated at
m
is denoted
\mathcal{R}_{m}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, and
\mathcal{R}_{m,+}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
denotes its subset of non-negative elements.

Lemma 5
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\bm{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
. The following propositions are equivalent:

(i)

\forall g\in\mathcal{R}_{m,+}(\mathbb{R}_{+}),\;\braket{f_{\bm{s}},g}\geq 0
,

(ii)

A_{\bm{s}}\succeq 0
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4
, any non-negative polynomial over
\mathbb{R}_{+}
of degree less or equal to
m
can be expressed as a sum of polynomials of the form
\sum_{l=0}^{m}x^{l}\sum_{i+j=2l}y_{i}y_{j}
, where
Y=(y_{0},\dots,y_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
. Hence, any non-negative truncated Laguerre series (the elements of
\mathcal{R}_{m,+}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
) can be expressed as as sum of terms of the form
e^{-\frac{x}{2}}\sum_{l=0}^{m}x^{l}\sum_{i+j=2l}y_{i}y_{j}
. By linearity, it is sufficient to check that the scalar products with these expressions are non-negative.

For all
k\in\mathbb{N}
we have

s_{k}=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\mathcal{L}_{k}(x)f_{\bm{s}}(x)dx.

(104)

Thus,

\displaystyle A_{\bm{s}}\succeq 0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\forall Y\in\mathbb{R}^{m+1},\;Y^{T}A_{\bm{s}}Y\geq 0

(105)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\forall Y\in\mathbb{R}^{m+1},\;\sum_{i,j=0}^{m}y_{i}y_{j}(A_{\bm{s}})_{ij}\geq 0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\forall Y\in\mathbb{R}^{m+1},\;\sum_{l=0}^{m}\sum_{i+j=2l}^{m}y_{i}y_{j}\sum_{k=0}^{l}s_{k}\binom{l}{k}l!\geq 0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\forall Y\in\mathbb{R}^{m+1},\;\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\sum_{l=0}^{m}\sum_{i+j=2l}^{m}y_{i}y_{j}\sum_{k=0}^{l}\binom{l}{k}l!\mathcal{L}_{k}(x)f_{\bm{s}}(x)dx\geq 0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\forall Y\in\mathbb{R}^{m+1},\;\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\sum_{l=0}^{m}\sum_{i+j=2l}^{m}y_{i}y_{j}\sum_{k=0}^{l}(-1)^{k}\binom{l}{k}l!L_{k}(x)e^{-\frac{x}{2}}f_{\bm{s}}(x)dx\geq 0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\forall Y\in\mathbb{R}^{m+1},\;\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\left(e^{-\frac{x}{2}}\sum_{l=0}^{m}x^{l}\sum_{i+j=2l}^{m}y_{i}y_{j}\right)f_{\bm{s}}(x)dx\geq 0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\forall Y\in\mathbb{R}^{m+1},\;\left\langle f_{\bm{s}},x\mapsto e^{-\frac{x}{2}}\sum_{l=0}^{m}x^{l}\sum_{i+j=2l}^{m}y_{i}y_{j}\right\rangle\geq 0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\forall g\in\mathcal{R}_{m,+}(\mathbb{R}_{+}),\;\braket{f_{\bm{s}},g}\geq 0,

where we used Eq. (
104
) in the fourth line and Eq. (
80
) in the sixth line.
∎

Appendix D
